# Introduction

1. The Hong Kong Code on Takeovers and Mergers was first introduced in 1975 and, after amendments from time to time in detailed respects, was thoroughly reviewed between 1989 and 1991, resulting in the current version being published in 1992. A new Rule 2.10 on privatisations by scheme of arrangement was introduced in 1993 and there have been some further minor amendments. The SFC has carried out a review of the Code in the light of the experience gained in the five years since its publication. Although the structure and conditions of the Hong Kong market differ greatly from those of London, much of the London experience remains relevant. The London experience has therefore been taken into account in this review.

2. The review has been carried out by the Special Adviser to the SFC, Mr. Christopher de Boer, former Chairman of the Takeovers and Mergers Panel in Hong Kong, in conjunction with the Corporate Finance Division of the SFC. The draft revised Code has been endorsed by the Takeovers Panel and the Commission. The draft revised Code, which is now presented for public consultation and comment, has been approved for that purpose by the SFC and the public is invited to provide comments.

3. In general, the draft revised Code reflects the view that, in its basic object of protecting public shareholders when control of their company changes, the main structure of the existing Code is sound, although in need of the addition of interpretive guidance. No changes are proposed to the administration of the Code or to its voluntary nature. Although the Panel has been subject to Court proceedings on several occasions it is still the case that a voluntary system has the advantages of flexibility and adaptability, without interfering with or delaying the takeover process. The continuance of a voluntary Code can only be assured if those concerned with takeovers demonstrate a consistent willingness to comply with rulings made by the Executive and the Panel. This aspect will be kept under continuing review.

4. The most significant issue under review has been Rule 2.10 and the voting requirements for privatisations by scheme of arrangement. This is described in detail below.

5. The following sections of the Consultation Paper comprise a summary of the main changes to the Code, a description of the options in relation to Rule 2.10, and a more detailed commentary on the other changes. (Please see **Appendix 2** for all detailed proposed amendments to the current Code.)

6. The Share Repurchase Code, which was introduced in November 1992, is not presently the subject of review, but it is intended that a full review would be undertaken later this year.

1


---

# Summary

The principal points reviewed and changes recommended are:

(a) The Code would remain a voluntary system although this would be kept under review.

(b) The essential structure of the Code would not be changed, although it would be refined to take account of relevant experience in Hong Kong and London.

(c) The "trigger point" would remain at 35% and the annual "creeper" would remain at 5% in any 12 month period. There have been no strong representations that either should be changed and the factors which applied at the last review remain valid. There are different views among members of the Panel and these two issues will be reviewed later this year.

(d) In light of the experience since Rule 2.10 was introduced, and in particular the result of two proposed privatisations in 1997 which narrowly failed to achieve the required level of approval, the Panel has considered several options as described in this paper. These include (i) maintaining the Rule in its current form, (ii) reducing the level of approval required of independent shareholders to 75%, (iii) changing the Rule so that a privatisation scheme can only be blocked by (in addition to any requirements in law) (1) more than 10% of the independent shareholders voting in person or by proxy against the proposal **and** (2) those independent shareholders holding more than 2.5% of the total voting rights of the company, and (iv) removing the requirement that the terms of the scheme must, in the opinion of an independent financial adviser, be fair and reasonable. These options are discussed in more detail in the next section.

(e) New definitions of "associate" and "derivative" have been included.

(f) A new Whitewash Guidance Note has been added to a revised Note 1 on Dispensations from Rule 26 to set out clearly which Rules of the Code apply to such transactions.

(g) Notes 6 and 7 to Rule 26.1 have been amended to clarify the circumstances in which transfers of shares between members of a concert party or to new members may give rise to an obligation for a general offer to be made.

(h) Rule 2.2 has been changed to cover only the voting position of concert parties at shareholder meetings to approve delistings, and Notes 3 and 4 to Rule 2 have been deleted.

(i) Notes have been added to the definitions of acting in concert and associate, and to Rule 22, to allow advisers to apply to the Executive for exemption for other members of their group from the presumption of acting in concert and from the disclosure of dealings requirements when the Executive is satisfied as to the Chinese Wall and

3


---

other arrangements in the group. These exemptions may be on a case by case basis or on a continuing basis.

(j) The Notes to Rules 23, 24 and 26.3 have been changed to clarify the treatment of purchases and conversion or exercise of convertibles, warrants and options. It is the conversion or exercise which is relevant, regardless of when the convertibles, warrants or options were purchased and the price will be determined by reference to the purchase price and the relevant conversion or exercise terms. In Rule 26.3 the market price on the day of conversion or exercise may be relevant if the convertibles, warrants or options were issued privately to the purchaser.

Any person wishing to comment on the recommendations is asked to do so in writing. Comments should be received by the SFC prior to the close of business on 20 March 1998 and should be addressed to:

The Securities and Futures Commission

## Rule 2.10 - Privatisation by scheme of arrangement

There are widely differing views in the market about the requirements of this Rule, which were the subject of public consultation in 1992, for 90% approval by independent shareholders and for an opinion from the independent financial adviser that the terms of the privatisation are fair and reasonable. The arguments for and against the Rule were set out in the 1992 consultation paper (a copy of which is attached as **Appendix 1**) and have not changed to any significant extent.

The principal reason for the introduction of the Rule was to give public shareholders a greater say in whether certain privatisations are successful and redressing the balance between a controlling shareholder and the public shareholders. This would be likely to lead to higher prices being offered by controlling shareholders to give themselves a greater chance of achieving success. Against this it was argued that it would give too much power to a holder or holders of a very small percentage of the company to block a proposal and increase the chance of greenmail; in addition it would significantly reduce the number of privatisation proposals as controlling shareholders would not be willing to take the risk of being blocked by a very small percentage who for their own reasons did not support a fair and reasonable offer.

There has now been nearly five years of experience of the operation of the Rule and its proponents and critics can both derive support for their views from this experience. In particular the experience in two unsuccessful proposed privatisations during 1997, of the Kwong Sang Hong International Limited ("KSH") and Tristate Holdings Limited ("Tristate"), where approval of the proposals was 87.5% and 83.3% respectively, has brought the debate to a head.

In the KSH proposal Peregrine Investments Holdings Limited held approximately 52% of KSH when the privatisation was announced. 39.7% of the public shareholders voted on the scheme of which 87.5% voted in favour. The 12.5% who voted against the scheme represented 2.38% of the total issued share capital.

In the Tristate proposal the Tang family held approximately 66% of Tristate when the privatisation was announced. 41.7% of the public shareholders voted on the scheme of which 83.3% voted in favour. The 16.7% who voted against the scheme represented 2.37% of the total issued share capital.

In all there have been 12 privatisations by scheme of arrangement proposed since Rule 2.10 was introduced of which 7 have been successful. In 6 of these cases the votes in favour of the scheme were over 99% and the other was 92.8%. Of the 3 unsuccessful proposals, apart from KSH and Tristate discussed above, one was approved by 99.9% of the public shareholders but was voted down by the warrantholders, and the other 2 achieved 65% and 12% approval respectively. The percentage of public shareholders voting on the schemes has varied from 29% to 69% with the average being approximately 48%. The percentage held by the controlling shareholder has varied between 52% and 75% with the average being 66%.

5


---

In addition to the options of leaving the Rule as it is or changing the percentage required to 75%, as is currently required under relevant laws in the various relevant jurisdictions, the Panel has considered other percentages in between these two levels but there is no logic to any other level except as a mathematical variation. The Panel has therefore looked at other formulae. If a shareholder holding 75% wished to privatise and every public shareholder voted, a holder of over 2.5% of the total shares could block the resolution. Alternatively if a shareholder holding 50% wished to privatise and only 50% of the public shareholders voted, again the holder of just over 2.5% could block it. Using the average percentages in the paragraph above, if a shareholder holding 66% wished to privatise and 48% voted on the scheme, a holder of 1.632% could block it under the current Rule. In order to allow for the inertia of shareholders in voting at general meetings, which may allow the holders of a small percentage of the voting rights to unduly influence the results of a meeting, the Panel has considered introducing a further test which would require a specific percentage of shareholders to vote against a proposal if the 90% level is not reached. As can be seen from the percentages above both KSH and Tristate would have been successful if this test had been in place at a level of 2.5%.

In addition to the level of voting the Panel has also considered the requirement for an opinion from the independent adviser that the offer is fair and reasonable. It has been argued that this requirement is unnecessary, particularly if the voting level is 90%; provided an independent adviser gives an opinion on the offer terms, it is for the shareholders to decide. Doubts have also been expressed because the requirement may lead to a controlling shareholder "shopping" for opinions; it was felt originally that this requirement would not be acceptable on its own without the 90% voting level for this reason. The majority of the Panel believe that this requirement should be retained whatever the voting level as it does provide an extra level of comfort for public shareholders.

The options considered by the Panel therefore are:

(i) to keep the Rule as it is;
(ii) to lower the voting approval requirement to 75%;
(iii) to keep the Rule as it is but to add a further test, if the 90% approval is not achieved, so that more than 2.5% of the total voting rights of the company must vote against the proposal for it to fail (or a higher percentage);
(iv) to remove from any of the above options the requirement for a fair and reasonable opinion from the financial adviser.

If option (iii) was adopted, the proposed wording of paragraph (a) of Rule 2.10 would be as follows:

"(a) in addition to satisfying any voting requirements imposed by law, the scheme of arrangement must be either

> (i) approved by a majority in number representing 90% in value of those shares that are voted either in person or by proxy at a duly convened general meeting by shareholders other than the person seeking to privatise the company and persons acting in concert with him, or

6

(ii) if not so approved by the requisite majority, not disapproved by shareholders voting in person or by proxy at such general meeting holding more than 2.5% of the total number of shares in issue;"

The SFC considers that there are no compelling reasons, based on the experience of the operation of this Rule, to lower the voting threshold to 75%. The offer prices in the 12 privatisation proposals which have been made since the Rule was introduced have been higher on average than the prices in such proposals in the three years before its introduction, based on the premium to the market price of the offeree company shares. Although there have been fewer proposals since the introduction of the Rule than in the previous period, it is not clear to what extent this was a function of different market conditions; fewer privatisations would be expected in a strongly rising market. It is also impossible to assess how many proposals were considered but abandoned because of the 90% voting requirement. The SFC has some sympathy with the argument that a very small minority can block a fair and reasonable proposal and therefore considers option (iii) to be a viable option.

Although comments are invited on any aspect of the above options, the SFC is particularly interested in receiving comments on the following issues:

(a) Which of the above options do you prefer? Do you think there are better options?

(b) The principal objective of Rule 2.10 is to give public shareholders a greater say in whether privatisations effected by way of schemes of arrangement should be successful. Do you think this objective has been achieved after implementation of this Rule in April 1993? Do you agree that this objective should continue to be pursued?

(c) Do you see any negative or undesirable effects of Rule 2.10 which have not been discussed in the 1992 consultation paper or in this paper and which would suggest that Rule 2.10 should be removed?

(d) Do you have any comments on the failure of the two privatisations of KSH and Tristate where the proposals received 87.5% and 83.3% approval by independent shareholders respectively?

(e) Do you think option (iii) is a good way to address the concern that holders of a small percentage of the voting rights may block a privatisation, particularly if the percentage of public shareholders voting is low? Do you think 2.5% is an appropriate level for the minimum shareholding requirement to block a privatisation by scheme of arrangement? Would you suggest a higher or lower percentage level?

(f) Do you think the current requirement for a fair and reasonable opinion from the independent financial adviser provides more protection for public shareholders? Do you share an observation that such requirement for fair and reasonable opinion may encourage opinion shopping? Do you think the requirement is unnecessary whether the voting level is 90% or 75%?

7


---

Commentary

## PART 1 : INTRODUCTION

**1.1 Section 1 - Nature and purpose of the Code** - In Section 1.7 a new paragraph has been added to emphasise that financial advisers must be able to satisfy the Executive that they have the professional competence and adequate resources to fulfil their role and discharge their responsibilities under the Code. This follows the judgment in a case before the Court in 1997 in which it was held that the Executive was entitled to be satisfied on these points.

**1.2 Section 8 - Consulting the Executive** - A new Section 8.2 has been added to explain in more detail the procedures adopted by the Executive in dealing with consultations and rulings. Generally consultations are verbal discussions which are not concluded with any formal and written ruling: views expressed by the Executive under consultations are preliminary and do not bind the Executive as rulings which are subject to review by the Panel on application.

**1.3 Section 16 - Meetings and hearings of the Panel** - This Section has been expanded to set out more clearly the procedures adopted by the Panel at its meetings or hearings. It is proposed that no more than two representatives of a party's financial adviser or solicitor may attend.

**1.4 Section 18 - Publication of rulings** - This Section has been expanded to clarify the process by which rulings are announced to the parties and the public, and to emphasise that no announcements should be made by any of the parties before the Panel's announcement is published.

## PART 2 : CODE ON TAKEOVERS AND MERGERS

### DEFINITIONS

**2.1 Acting in concert** - Wording has been added before the list of persons presumed to be acting in concert to draw attention to the fact that a concert party may exist through other relationships.

2.2 A new Note 4 has been introduced referring to investors in a consortium formed for the purpose of making an offer normally being treated as acting in concert with the offeror.

2.3 A new Note 5 has been added to allow an adviser to apply to the Executive for exemption for another member of their group from the presumption of acting in concert, either on a case by case basis, as has been the practice of the Executive, or on a continuing basis. The exemption would be based on information from the adviser on various aspects of the group, including the shareholding structure, the Chinese Wall arrangements, any common directorships, the fund management activities and

9


---

compliance arrangements. The exemption would be conditional on the information continuing to be valid, any changes being notified to the Executive and periodic review by the Executive.

2.4 **Associate** - A new definition of associate has been included to avoid the confusion which may arise between persons who have an obligation under Rule 22 to disclose dealings in relevant securities ( who are now defined as associates) and persons acting in concert with the offeror, who have significantly greater obligations. Rule 22 has been amended accordingly. No changes of principle have been made except to the extent that parties who were previously able to rebut the presumption of acting in concert would now be required to disclose their dealings as associates while still not acting in concert. A note similar to that described in 2.3 above will be included, allowing exemption from disclosure of dealing requirements to be applied for.

2.5 **Convertibles, Options and Warrants** - New definitions have been added to explain that references to these instruments include those convertible or exercisable into both new and existing shares.

2.6 **Derivative** - In 1996 the Takeover Panel in London introduced the definition of derivative to cover financial products which do not include the possibility of delivery of underlying relevant securities. While these have not yet posed any problems in Hong Kong it is considered prudent to be prepared. The requirements essentially relate to disclosure in announcements and offer documents of holdings and dealings in such products. Consequential amendments have been made in the following Rules: the Definitions section, Rule 3, Note to Rule 8, Rule 21.4, Rule 22 and Schedule I.

2.7 **New Note 2 - References to bank or financial advisers** - This Note clarifies the circumstances in which references to bank or financial advisers do not apply.

## RULES

2.8 **Rule 1 - Approach** - The heading of Rule 1.1 has been changed for greater accuracy.

2.9 **Rule 2 - Independent advice, independent committees and shareholder approval** - In Rule 2.4 the concept of materiality in the conflict of interest which a director of an offeror might face has been introduced. There is also a cross-reference to Rule 26.2(b) to draw the attention of potential offerors under Rule 26 to the requirements under this Rule to obtain shareholder consent to an offer before an obligation is incurred under Rule 26. A new Note on timing of advice has also been added to point out that an offeror board must obtain independent advice before announcing an offer.

2.10 **Rule 2.2 and Notes 3 and 4 - Approval of privatisations and similar proposals, statutory rights of compulsory acquisition** - Rule 2.2 now covers only the restriction on voting by concert parties at shareholder meetings to approve Stock Exchange delistings. The Rule was originally introduced after one transaction to deal

---

with proposals which had the effect of privatisations. Since then the Stock Exchange Listing Rules have been changed to cover such situations and the need for the current Rule in the Code has gone. Notes 3 and 4 to Rule 2 have therefore also been deleted.

2.11 **Notes to Rule 2** - These Notes will be moved to after Rule 2.10. Note 6 has been amended to cover the situation where there is a divergence of views between members of a board or between the board and the independent adviser, in which case an explanation must be given, including the arguments for acceptance or rejection.

2.12 **Rule 3 - Announcements** - The Panel is of the view that Rule 3, in its current form, is not satisfactory in that it does not deal with the situation where prior to an approach the vendor should be responsible for making an announcement. Rules 3.1 and 3.2 have therefore been rewritten into three Rules to clearly define when announcements are required from an offeror or potential offeror, the offeree board, and a potential vendor respectively. There is a new requirement, in new Rule 3.2(d), for an announcement when the board of an offeree is aware that there are negotiations or discussions between a potential offeror and the holder or holders of 35% or more of the voting rights of the company, or when the board of the company is seeking potential offerors and there are rumours or undue share price movements. There is also a requirement in new Rule 3.3 for an announcement by a potential vendor when the board of the offeree company is unaware of such discussions.

2.13 **Notes to old Rule 3.4** - A new Note 2 has been added to explain that, while it would not be prudent to enquire about the holdings of other parts of an adviser's group to be included in the announcement of an offer, details of such holdings should be sought immediately after the announcement and the Executive consulted to decide whether a further announcement is required. In current Note 4 it is stated that the Executive may also require evidence that the offeror has sufficient financial resources to complete the purchase of shares which gives rise to the obligation.

2.14 **Rule 7 - Obligations of directors** - The cross-reference in the Note to the Rule has been amended to be consistent with Rule 26.4.

2.15 **Rule 8 - Timing and content of documents** - The Panel is concerned at the number of occasions on which offer documents are not despatched within the 21 days set out in Rule 8.2, and also the despatch of the offeree board circular within 14 days of the posting of the offer document under Rule 8.4. This could result in offeree shareholders not receiving advice from the offeree board and its advisers before the first closing date. It is not proposed to alter the time limits for posting of these documents but parties are encouraged in agreed transactions to produce composite documents so as to give offeree shareholders full information as soon as practicable. Appropriate amendments have been made to Rule 8.2. and in Rule 8.4 the Executive's consent is required if there is to be a delay in posting the offeree board circular.

2.16 **Rule 9 - Standard of care and responsibility** - Several paragraphs have been added to the Note to this Rule to explain more clearly the procedures which directors may adopt to delegate responsibility for day-to-day conduct of an offer to a committee

10                                                                                                                    11


---

of the board while emphasising the continued joint and several responsibility of the directors.

2.17 **Rule 10 - Profit forecasts** - The Rule and the associated Notes have been expanded to give more guidance particularly in relation to the assumptions on which the forecast is based. Under new Rule 10.9 the Executive may require a working capital statement to be reported on, if such a statement is required by the Listing Rules.

2.18 **Rule 11 - Asset valuations** - Three new Notes to this Rule have been added, to explain the types of asset to which the Rule applies, to refer to property in developing property markets and Practice Note 12 of the Listing Rules, and to refer to the Guidance Notes of the relevant professional bodies. Current Note 2, Companies with significant property interests, has been changed by dropping the $50 million criterion of materiality.

2.19 **Rule 12 - Issuance of documents** - The number of copies of documents to be filed with the Executive has been changed to reflect current practice.

2.20 **Rule 13 - Appropriate offers for convertibles, warrants, etc** - Rule 13.3 has been amended to refer to the Rule applying to non-transferable options. The Note to the Rule codifies the Panel decision in the Asean Resources case that the "see-through" price should be regarded as the minimum price.

2.21 **Rule 15 - Offer timetable** - Rule 15.4 has been expanded to deal with certain announcements after day 39.

2.22 **Rule 21 - Restrictions on dealings before and during the offer** - A new paragraph has been added to Rule 21.2 so that the consent of the Executive is not required for placing or underwriting arrangements made during an offer in order to achieve the minimum public shareholding to maintain the offeree's listing, but consent is required if the offeror as a result of the arrangements wishes to hold less than 75% of the offeree company.

2.23 **Rule 22 - Disclosure of dealings during the offer period** - With the definition of associate this Rule has been rewritten and the Notes have been expanded to give more details of the requirements and terms of disclosure.

2.24 **Rule 23 - When cash offer required** - Rule 23(a) has been changed so that a cash offer is required when shares of any class under offer carrying 10% or more of the voting rights exercisable at a class meeting of that class are acquired for cash by an offeror rather than when such shares carry 10% or more of the voting rights of the company. The last paragraph of Rule 23 has been rewritten to make it clear that it applies to that Rule only.

2.25 **Notes to Rule 23** - The wording of Note 3 has been changed to explain that the conversion or exercise of convertibles, warrants or options will be treated as purchases of the underlying securities at a price calculated by reference to the purchase price and the relevant conversion or exercise price. The wording of Note 4 has been changed to make it clear that it applies to Rule 23(c) and that the time period of purchases is during the offer period and the 6 months prior to its commencement. A new paragraph has been added to explain that Rule 23(c) may also be relevant when 10% or more has been acquired for a mixture of cash and securities. A new Note 5 has been added to set out some of the factors which the Executive might take into account in considering an application for an adjusted price.

2.26 **Rule 24 - Purchases at above offer price** - Wording has been added in Rule 24.1 to make it clear that the offer price means the then current value of the offer. Rule 24.2 has been changed so that the calculation of the value of the offer should be by reference to the weighted average traded price of board lots. Rule 24.3 has been redrafted to make it clear that an immediate announcement of a revised offer must be made after a purchase of securities at above the offer price. A new Note 2 has been added to clarify the application of the Rule to the conversion or exercise of convertibles, warrants and options on the same terms as described in new Note 3 to Rule 23 above. A new Note 3 has been added to explain that subscription for new securities at a price above the offer price will be treated as a purchase unless the issue of the securities has been approved by shareholders under Rule 4.

2.27 **Note 2 to Rule 26.1 - Shareholders coming together to act in concert** - The Panel considered whether to change this Note as was proposed in the 1992 Consultation Paper but finally not implemented. On balance the Panel decided to leave the Note as it currently reads, except that it is proposed to change the wording to refer to where a party has acquired shares independently of other shareholders rather than without their knowledge.

2.28 **Notes 6 and 7 to Rule 26.1 - Acquisitions of voting rights by members of a group acting in concert and vendor of part only of a shareholding** - Note 6 has been amended to make it clear that if a single member of a concert party comes to hold 35% or more, or holding 35% or more acquires more than 5% in any 12 month period, that person would normally be required to make a general offer. It also sets out the factors to be taken into account by the Executive in determining whether to grant a waiver. Note 7 has been expanded so as to cover a wider range of potential arrangements between the vendor and the purchaser which might be designed to allow the purchaser to avoid a general offer obligation under the Rule, where the purchaser has effective control over the remaining shares held by the vendor. This could apply to a purchaser who was already acting in concert with the vendor as well as a new member of the group.

2.29 **Note 14 to Rule 26.1 - The 5% creeper - placing and top-up transactions** - This Note has been changed so that a placing shareholder will be deemed to have a lowest percentage holding equal to the lower of the lowest percentage holding which the placing shareholder had in the 12 month period prior to or immediately after the placing and top-up transaction. The Note has been extended to cover a transaction which does not give rise to an offer under the Rule but which complies with the requirements of Notes 6 and 7 of the Notes on dispensations from Rule 26.

---

# Page 14

**2.30 Rule 26.3 - Consideration** - Rule 26.3(a) has been amended to make it clear that the relevant period for determining the highest price paid is during the offer period and within the 6 months prior to its announcement, and the last clause has been deleted as it is no longer relevant. Note 2(c) has been changed so that, if voting rights have been acquired by the exercise of conversion rights or warrants, the price will normally be determined by reference to the purchase price and the relevant conversion or exercise terms, regardless of when the convertible securities or warrants were acquired. The market price on the day of exercise might however be relevant if the convertible securities or warrants were issued or sold privately to the purchaser.

**2.31 Note 1 on dispensations from Rule 26 - Vote of independent shareholders on the issue of new securities ( Whitewashes )** - The current Note has been split between a shorter summary Note and a new Guidance Note, which sets out in detail the Rules of the Code which apply to a whitewash transaction. There are no major changes in the requirements from those currently applied by the Executive.

**2.32 Notes 6 and 7 of the Notes on dispensations from Rule 26 - Placing and top-up transactions and verification of independence of placees** - Note 6 now explains that a waiver is not required when a shareholder who together with persons acting in concert with him holds 50% or more of the voting rights carries out a placing and top-up transaction. The Note also applies to top-ups up to the original shareholding held by the placing shareholder. Note 7 highlights the particular concern that the Executive will have in verifying the independence of a single placee.

**2.33 Rule 31.3 - 6 months delay before acquisition above offer price** - The wording of the Rule has been changed and a new Note added to make it clear that the issue of new shares does not need the consent of the Executive under this Rule. Another Note has been added to cover the acquisition of convertibles, warrants or options at an effective price above the offer price which may be relevant under this Rule.

**2.34 Rule 33 - Disclosure of acquisitions of 10% and above** - The disclosure required under this Rule must now contain the price at which voting rights are acquired or disposed of and must be made to the Executive and the Stock Exchange as well as the company. Accordingly Note 2 has been deleted as the SDIO disclosure does not require the price to be disclosed. All references to shares have been changed to voting rights.

**2.35 Schedules I and II - Contents of offer document and offeree board circular** - A cash flow statement will now be required to be disclosed in the offer document (in the case of a securities exchange offer) and in the offeree board circular. It is proposed that all offer documents must now contain a description of how the offer is to be financed and the source of the finance, with the principal lenders or arrangers being named. Where the offeror intends that the payment of interest on, repayment of or security for any liability ( contingent or otherwise) will depend to any significant extent on the business of the offeree company, a description of the arrangements contemplated will be required.

October 1992

# Public Consultation
## Amendments to the Codes on Takeovers and Mergers and Share Repurchases In Relation to Schemes of Arrangement

## EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

The SFC is considering amendments to the Takeovers and Share Repurchase Codes to enhance investor protection in the context of certain privatisation transactions. The proposal applies only to privatisations effected by way of schemes of arrangement (as compared to those effected by way of a general offer coupled with the compulsory acquisition procedure) and is not likely to have an adverse impact on the majority of scheme proposals which traditionally have been properly utilised.

The primary objective of the reform proposal is to strengthen the bargaining position of minority shareholders in certain privatisation transactions and, by doing so, provide an incentive for such privatisation offers to be on terms that are fair and reasonable to shareholders. The paper notes that any reform proposal in this area must be careful not to impede commercial activity or involve excessive regulatory intervention.

The paper outlines the existing procedures in relation to privatisations and then outlines alternatives for reform. In summary, the proposal would amend the Codes to provide that all takeovers and share repurchases are to be conducted by way of a general offer (i.e. which would include privatisations by way of compulsory acquisition). The proposal goes on to provide that the Executive will normally waive the general offer obligation (i.e. permit privatisations by scheme of arrangement), provided the scheme proposal is:

(i) subject to the prior approval by 90% (as compared to the current 75%) in value of the offeree's shareholders who are not acting in concert with the offeror and who are present and voting in person or by proxy;

(ii) a fair and reasonable proposal in the opinion of an independent financial adviser; and

(iii) conducted in accordance with other applicable laws and regulations.

The SFC is inviting comments on this proposal. Comments should be received by November 6, 1992.

17


---

# 1. Background & Objective

1.1 The SFC's statutory duties include taking all reasonable steps to safeguard the interests of investors in securities and suggesting reforms of the law relating to securities. Through the Codes on Takeovers and Mergers and Share Repurchases the SFC seeks to protect the interests of minority shareholders in takeover, merger and share repurchase transactions.

1.2 The SFC is considering whether to amend the Codes to address investor protection concerns associated with the use of schemes of arrangement for the purpose of effecting takeover, merger and share repurchase transactions. Such consideration is being made in response to concerns that have been expressed by investors, the media and other interested parties about some proposals to privatise listed companies by way of a scheme of arrangement and in the light of the experience with schemes of arrangement in Hong Kong. A "privatisation" involves the acquisition of 100% of the shares of a listed or public company through a transaction where all minority shareholders (including those who decline to sell or who vote against the transaction because they consider the offer price to be inadequate) can be compelled to dispose of their shares to the offeror.

1.3 The principal objective of the proposal is to give minority shareholders a greater say in whether certain privatisations are successful. The proposal has been endorsed by the Takeovers and Mergers Panel and the SFC for release for public comment.

1.4 To put this matter in perspective, it should be acknowledged that privatisations are not inherently abusive and that the majority of schemes of arrangement have been legitimate both in terms of how they have been conducted and in terms of their commercial objectives. This proposal is not likely to have a significant impact on schemes that are fair and reasonable to minority shareholders.

1.5 In presenting this proposal, the SFC acknowledges that reform proposals must be careful not to unnecessarily impede commercial activity or involve excessive regulatory intervention. However, the SFC's experience in relation to the processes by which the shareholdings of minority shareholders are statutorily expropriated indicates that the rules for protecting minorities need to be brought into alignment with the unique realities of this market. In other markets, a greater level of investor protection is achieved as a consequence of factors such as more organised and activist investors (particularly institutional investors), a greater preponderance of independent directors of widely held companies, easier access to the legal system (e.g. class actions, developed body of case law) or greater regulatory intervention. The closely-held nature of Hong Kong companies, the limited development of the concept of independent directors, the foreign domicile phenomenon, the lack of an organized and activist institutional base, among others, means that the protections available in certain other markets do not exist in Hong Kong. The proposal is presented in light of this need to balance the rights of majority and minority shareholders.

# 2. Existing Privatisation Procedures & Investor Protection Concerns

2.1 **Companies Ordinance** - Under Hong Kong company law, privatisations have generally been effected in one of two ways:

   (a) by way of a **general offer** followed by a **compulsory acquisition** pursuant to section 168 and the Ninth Schedule of the Companies Ordinance; or

   (b) by way of a **scheme of arrangement** pursuant to section 166 of the Companies Ordinance.

   Almost one-half of the companies listed in Hong Kong are now incorporated overseas, primarily in Bermuda. The company law in Bermuda regarding privatisations is substantially the same as Hong Kong law.

2.2 **General Offer/Compulsory Acquisition** - Section 168 and the Ninth Schedule allow an offeror making a takeover offer for the shares of an offeree company to privatise the offeree company if it can acquire **nine-tenths in value of the shares for which the general offer is made** within the period of four months beginning on the date of the offer. Under such circumstances the offeror may give notice to any shareholder who does not accept the offer that the offeror wishes to acquire the shareholder's shares and, on giving the notice, the offeror becomes entitled and bound to acquire the shares on the terms of the offer. **An offeror is thus required to acquire 90% of the shares which are the subject of the offer before he can effectively compel the remaining holders to sell their shares without their consent.** The 90% threshold is intended to represent a level of acceptance which demonstrates that the offer is sufficiently attractive to shareholders that it is reasonable to coerce the remaining dissenting shareholders to sell their shares. Thus, if an offer is made that is overwhelmingly accepted, section 168 allows an offeror to achieve its objective of privatising the offeree company in order to avoid the cost and inconvenience that would otherwise be associated with a continuing minority interest. The company law of the United Kingdom, Australia, Canada and Bermuda also contains compulsory acquisition provisions that are comparable to section 168.

18 | 19


---

# Page 20

**2.3 Schemes of Arrangement** - Section 166 of the Ordinance sets out a means by which a company can enter into an arrangement with its members or creditors to alter its capital structure by consolidating shares of different classes and/or by dividing shares into shares of different classes. If a company proposes to effect a restructuring by way of an arrangement with its members, or any class of them, it may apply to the court for an order directing that a meeting of the members be convened, in such manner as the court directs, to consider the arrangement proposal. To become effective, the arrangement must be approved at the meeting by a majority in number representing three-fourths in value of the members, or class of members (i.e. independent shareholders), who are present and voting at the meeting either in person or by proxy. If approved at the meeting, the arrangement must be sanctioned by the court whereupon it becomes binding on the members and the company. The company law of the United Kingdom, Australia, Bermuda and Canada also contains scheme of arrangement provisions that are comparable to section 166.

**2.4 Schemes Common in Hong Kong** - In Hong Kong, schemes of arrangement are often used as a way of making takeover bids to privatise companies. They usually take the form of a proposal to cancel all shares of the offeree company, other than those held by the offeror, in exchange for cash or shares of the offeror. Schemes of arrangement are primarily used to make a takeover bid when the offeree company is prepared to cooperate to seek the approval of its members and the court for a capital restructuring which will meet the offeror's objectives. They are generally unsuited to hostile takeover bids and are more common in Hong Kong than elsewhere because many public companies in Hong Kong are closely held. The scheme route may be less expensive overall than the general offer route as stamp duty is avoided.

**2.5 Differences in Approval Thresholds** - An offeror can only privatise a company by using the compulsory acquisition provisions if the offeror can actually get acceptances for no less than 90% of the shares for which the offer is made. By contrast, an offeror that has the cooperation of an offeree company can privatise the offeree by way of a scheme of arrangement if it can secure approval for the scheme by a majority in number representing 75% in value of the independent shareholders of the offeree who are present and voting at the meeting either in person or by proxy. It is acknowledged that the courts have clearly approved the use of schemes to privatise companies. The proposals contained in this paper seek to reduce the existing difference between the effective shareholder approval thresholds for privatisations effected by means of compulsory acquisition provisions and privatisations effected by way of schemes of arrangement.

---

# Page 21

**2.6** Not only is the absolute approval threshold for schemes of arrangement 15% lower than the threshold for compulsory acquisitions, but the scheme threshold applies only with respect to those shareholders who are present and voting at the shareholders' meeting, while the compulsory acquisition threshold applies with respect to all shareholders to whom the offer is made. As a result, minority shareholders of an offeree company which is the subject of a scheme of arrangement that is effectively a takeover bid receive less protection in the context of what is clearly a connected transaction than they would receive if the privatisation attempt was made by an arm's length party in reliance upon compulsory acquisition provisions. Securities regulation normally seeks to achieve the opposite result to give minority shareholders relatively more say in relation to connected transactions.

**2.7 Court Approval** - Notwithstanding the significant difference between the approval thresholds applicable to general offers followed by compulsory acquisition and schemes of arrangement, the only extra safeguard for minority shareholders in schemes of arrangement is the need for court approval. But in cases where the requisite shareholder approvals have been obtained, and no one appears before the court to object to court approval of the scheme, such approval is generally a formality. In Hong Kong there has been little or no opposition before the Courts to proposals due, in part, to the costs and other procedural impediments associated with the making of related applications to the Court. Such costs and procedural impediments effectively deny Court access to most minority shareholders. Access to the courts is even more difficult in the case of overseas domiciled companies which now represent approximately 45% of all listed companies. It remains to be seen how a foreign court would handle a case where a foreign domiciled company sought to implement a scheme objected to by a number of Hong Kong shareholders.

**2.8 Effect of Schemes on Minorities' Bargaining Position** - As a result of the lower, 75% approval threshold for schemes, there is an incentive for an offeror to attempt to privatise a company by way of a scheme for a lower price than if he sought to privatise the company by way of a general offer in reliance upon compulsory acquisition provisions, where the approval threshold is 90%. In some cases, schemes of arrangement can allow offerors to privatise companies with offers which are not fair and reasonable. Furthermore, even though some scheme offers may not be fair and reasonable, schemes of arrangement allow the shares of a larger number of shareholders to be expropriated than could be expropriated by way of a compulsory acquisition resulting from an offer that was fair and reasonable.

# Page 22

## 3. Options for Reform

To put the reform process in perspective, several options were considered. The principal options and the reasons why they were not pursued further are summarised below.

### 3.1 Ban Privatisations Effected by Schemes

Pursuant to this option, privatisations effected by schemes would simply be prohibited and the offeror would be left to make a general offer and utilise the usual compulsory acquisition provisions. This would have the effect of requiring all privatisations to be accomplished by way of the statutory compulsory acquisition provisions - the method intended by legislators to be used for expropriation of minority interests. Such a prohibition was considered by some commentators to be an unnecessarily restrictive regulatory response given that privatisations effected by way of schemes are not inherently abusive of minority shareholders.

### 3.2 Enhance Role of Regulators in Court Approval Process

This option could be modelled from the approach taken in other jurisdictions where the relevant regulatory body takes an active role in the court approval process and, in effect, makes a value judgement about the fairness and reasonableness of the privatisation. This option recognises that, in Hong Kong, once the requisite shareholder approvals have been obtained, it is very rare for a shareholder to appear in court to object to approval of the scheme. This option was viewed as being too interventionist for the Hong Kong market and inconsistent with the current Code which does not involve itself with an assessment of the merits of an offer. The commercial merits of individual offers are left to shareholders. This option would place a considerably higher premium on the SFC's activist role in regulating securities and, among other concerns, would likely require the commitment of additional resources and its involvement in court proceedings in various other jurisdictions (especially Bermuda and Cayman Islands).

### 3.3 Require Only That an Independent Adviser Opine that Scheme is Fair and Reasonable

Pursuant to this option, the proposal for consideration detailed below would be restricted such that the Executive would normally grant a waiver from the general offer requirement provided that an independent financial adviser opined that the scheme was a fair and reasonable one. This option would not impose a higher approval standard. Therefore, the shareholder approval threshold under corporate law, that is, a majority in number representing three-fourths in value of the members present and voting in person or by proxy would be the only relevant threshold. This option has not been viewed positively because to

---

# Page 23

require only the independent advice that the scheme is fair and reasonable would provide an strong incentive for "opinion shopping". There is keen competition amongst Hong Kong's numerous financial advisers putting pressure on advisers to give the offeror the advice which is sought.

## 4. The Amendment under Consideration

### 4.1 Comparable Approval Standards for Schemes

The objective underlying the amendments being proposed is to increase the level of protection afforded to minority shareholders of a company that is subject to a privatisation effected by means of a scheme of arrangement so that it moves closer to the level of protection afforded to shareholders of a company that is the subject of a privatisation effected by means of a general offer. This objective would be met by amending the Takeovers Code to require all takeover and merger transactions to be effected by way of a **general offer**, subject to the Executive's discretion to waive compliance with the general offer requirement in appropriate circumstances, and **normally to waive** such requirement if the following conditions can be met:

(a) the offeror is subject to prior approval by 90% in value of the shareholders of the offeree company who are not acting in concert and who are present and voting, either in person or by proxy, at a related meeting of the shareholders of the offeree company;

(b) in the opinion of an independent financial adviser the offer is a fair and reasonable one; and

(c) the offer is otherwise conducted in accordance with applicable laws and regulatory requirements.

The Executive would also consider waiving the general offer requirement in other circumstances, such as where the offeror was not a connected party and had no other connection (financial or otherwise) with the offeree company or its substantial shareholders and directors. Attached as **Appendix A** is a list of amendments to the Codes which would be made to give effect to the proposal.

### 4.2 Acting in Concert

The proposed amendments include a proposal to amend the definition of the term "acting in concert" by the addition of the phrase "by any of them" in the phrase "through the acquisition .... of voting rights of the company". Although Rule 26.1(b) and (d) refer to the acquisition of voting rights "by any one or more" persons acting in concert, it is appropriate to clarify the definition of "acting in

---

*Page 22 — Page 23*


---

concert". The term "acting in concert" would therefore more clearly catch persons who assist a controlling shareholder with its privatisation bid by using the votes attached to the shares they already have but who do not acquire more voting rights for the purpose. This phrase was chosen rather than requiring that the requisite majority should be a majority of "independent shareholders". The term "independent shareholders" was considered to be too restrictive because it would apply only to shareholders of a company which proposes to engage in a transaction who have no interest in the proposed transaction. As such it would not apply to shareholders whose shares would be cancelled as a result of a scheme of arrangement but who were assisting the controlling shareholder with its privatisation bid.

**4.3 Rationale for Solution** - As compared to the other options for reform described above, the proposal constitutes a practical and effective method to regulate schemes that are effectively takeover bids. It ensures that the regulation of such schemes will fall within the jurisdiction of the regulatory body charged with the responsibility for regulating takeovers, mergers and share repurchases in Hong Kong.

**4.4 Promoting Certainty and Flexibility** - Preliminary feedback from market practitioners indicates that there is a need for a proposal along these lines to provide for both certainty and flexibility. In relation to certainty, the three specific criteria described above would be the usual prerequisites for obtaining a waiver. In relation to flexibility, it is proposed to provide the Executive with a discretion to waive the general offer requirement and that the Executive will normally waive such requirement if the three waiver conditions can be met. As noted in 4.1 above, a waiver may also be available in other circumstances.

**4.5 Legal Rights** - The Takeovers Code cannot and does not seek to amend the law. Accordingly, the proposal would not interfere with legal rights arising under the Companies Ordinance. The primary purpose of the Takeovers Code is to afford fair treatment for shareholders who are affected by takeover and merger transactions. In order to achieve this, the Takeovers Code must, in some areas, impose upon those who wish to take advantage of the securities markets in Hong Kong, obligations which are additional to those provided for under the law. The proposal is designed to require those wishing to have access to the securities markets to comply with obligations which are considered to be in the interests of shareholders and of maintaining confidence in the fairness and integrity of those markets.

**4.6 How the Proposed Amendments Benefit Shareholders** - The proposed amendments benefit shareholders of companies that are the subject of a privatisation effected by means of a scheme of arrangement by providing a greater level of investor protection. As the approval threshold is intended to represent a level of acceptance which evidences the making of a fair and reasonable offer, the proposed amendments provide a heightened assurance that privatisation offers made using a scheme of arrangement will be fair and reasonable. The proposal should also tend to discourage privatisation bids that are not fair and reasonable.

## 5. Related Amendments to the Codes

**5.1 Repeal of Rule 2.3** - The recent amendments to the Takeovers Code which became effective on 1 April 1992 included a new Rule 2.3 which was intended to shift the costs associated with unsuccessful and unreasonable privatisation proposals from the offeree company to the shareholder seeking to privatise the company. Rule 2.3 provides that if a scheme offer is either not recommended by an offeree board's independent committee or is not recommended as fair and reasonable by the financial adviser to the independent committee, all expenses incurred by the offeree company in connection with the offer must be borne by the shareholder seeking to privatise the company if the scheme is not approved by shareholders. This rule would be repealed if the amendments to the Codes proposed by this memorandum are adopted, as all scheme offers will have to be found to be fair and reasonable by an independent financial adviser before being made.

**5.2 Share Repurchase General Offer Rule** - Under the Share Repurchase Code, a share repurchase must be conducted by way of a general offer or in accordance with the exemptions from the general offer requirement set out in Rule 2. Rule 2(g) prescribes an exemption from the general offer rule for schemes of arrangement that are approved or supervised by a court of law in Hong Kong. A public company in Hong Kong incorporated overseas would have to obtain a waiver from the Executive if it wished to conduct a scheme of arrangement tantamount to a share repurchase in accordance with the law of its jurisdiction of incorporation. A waiver from the general offer obligation would generally only be granted if it could be established that the scheme of arrangement legislation and the judicial supervision in the other jurisdiction was comparable to such legislation and judicial supervision in Hong Kong based upon investor protection considerations. For the reasons outlined below, the SFC is considering the replacement of the Rule 2(g) exemption with an analogous amendment to that presented in section 4.1 above.

---

---

## 5.3 Compulsory Acquisitions

Prior to 1 September 1991 companies incorporated under the Companies Ordinance were prohibited from repurchasing shares. Effective 1 September 1991 the Ordinance was amended to permit Hong Kong incorporated companies to engage in share repurchases. At the same time as the amendments became effective, the Thirteenth Schedule was added to the Ordinance. The Thirteenth Schedule is the share repurchase equivalent of the Ninth Schedule **compulsory acquisition** rules for takeover offers. Broadly speaking, the Thirteenth Schedule provides that if a company proposes to engage in a share repurchase offer and one or more of the shareholders of the company do not intend to tender their shares to the offer (because they wish to privatise the company), the company may give notice to shareholders of the company who do not accept the offer that it wishes to purchase their shares provided certain conditions are met, in particular that the repurchasing company acquires 90% of the shares for which the offer was made (excluding the shares held by the privatising shareholders) within the period of 4 months beginning on the date of the offer. Once the notice has been given, the repurchasing company becomes entitled and bound to purchase the remaining shares, other than those belonging to the privatising shareholders, on the terms of the offer (assuming there is no Court order to the contrary).

## 5.4 90% Approval Threshold

Like the compulsory acquisition provisions of the Ninth Schedule then, a repurchasing company must have acquired 90% of the shares which are the subject of its share repurchase offer before the privatising shareholders can privatise the company by effectively expropriating the remaining shares pursuant to the Thirteenth Schedule. Once again, like the section 168 compulsory acquisition procedures, **the 90% threshold is intended to represent a level of acceptance which evidences the making of a fair and reasonable offer**. Schemes that are tantamount to a share repurchase therefore give rise to the same problems as schemes that are effectively takeover bids, because both are presently characterised by the lower, 75% approval threshold. A significant difference between the two is that whereas offerors who privatise a company by way of a takeover bid, or the scheme equivalent thereof, use their own resources for such purpose, privatising shareholders are able to privatise a repurchasing company using the company's resources for such purpose.

## 5.5 Amendment under Consideration

Consistent with the reasoning in connection with the Takeovers Code, the SFC is considering amending the Share Repurchase Code by deleting the scheme of arrangement exemption prescribed by Rule 2(g) of the Code, and by providing that the Executive may waive the general offer share repurchase requirement prescribed by the Code in appropriate circumstances, and that it will normally waive such requirement if the waiver conditions set out in section 4.1 of this paper are met. Attached as **Appendix A** is a list of amendments to the Share Repurchase Code which would give effect to the proposal.

## 6. Request for Comments

6.1 Although comments are invited on any aspect of the proposal, the SFC is particularly interested in receiving comments on the following issues:

(a) The principal objective of the amendments under consideration is to give minority shareholders a greater say in whether privatisations effected by way of schemes of arrangement are successful. Do you agree that this objective should be pursued? Do you agree that **the regulations protecting the rights of minority shareholders in companies that are being privatised through the use of schemes of arrangement should be tightened?**

(b) In order to provide a higher level of protection for investors, the SFC has sought in the proposed amendments to reduce the present distinction between the effective shareholder approval thresholds for privatisations effected in reliance upon general offer/compulsory acquisition provisions and privatisations by way of schemes of arrangement, with the intended net result of strengthening the bargaining power of minority shareholders. **Do you agree that steps should be taken to bring the two thresholds for shareholder approval broadly in line with each other?**

(c) Referring to section 4.4 in the paper, there is a need for this proposal to provide for both certainty and flexibility. In relation to certainty, the three specific criteria are proposed as usual prerequisites for obtaining a waiver. In relation to flexibility, it is proposed to provide the Executive with a discretion to waive the general offer requirements and that the Executive will normally waive such requirements if the three waiver conditions can be met. **Do you agree with this approach? Is there a preferable approach?**

(d) **Do you agree with the proposed amendment to the definition of the term "acting in concert" in the context of the first waiver condition?**

(e) Rule 2.7 of the Takeovers Code already requires the board of an offeree company to obtain the opinion of an independent financial adviser as to whether the offer is, or is not, fair and reasonable. The second waiver condition (see section 4.1) would preclude offers from being conducted other than by way of a general offer if an independent financial adviser is unable to express an opinion that a proposed offer is a fair and reasonable one. Assuming a 90% shareholder approval threshold is required, is the second waiver condition necessary?

(f) In addition to the matters raised in this paper, do you think it is reasonable to preclude companies, controlling shareholders and directors who have breached either of the Codes from utilising schemes of arrangement for the purpose of privatising companies?

(g) In addition to the matters raised in this paper, do you think it is reasonable to preclude companies from utilising schemes to effect privatisations if the company does not have independent directors to assess the merits of a scheme?

### Takeovers Code

#### Definitions

**"Acting in concert :** Persons acting in concert comprise persons who, pursuant to an agreement or understanding, actively co-operate to obtain or consolidate "control" (as defined below) of a company through the acquisition by any of them of voting rights of the company."

**"General offer :** General offer means an offer made by an offeror on the same terms to each holder of securities that is the subject of the offer."

**"Offeree company :** Offeree company means a company that is the subject of a takeover or merger transaction."

**"Offeror :** Offeror means a person or persons who seek to control, or to consolidate control, of an offeree company."

**"Securities :** Securities has the meaning attributed to the term "securities" in the Securities Ordinance."

# 1. INTRODUCTION

1. **Nature and purpose of the Codes on Takeovers and Mergers and Share Repurchases**

    1.1 The Code on Takeovers and Mergers (the "Takeovers Code") and the Code on Share Repurchases (the "Share Repurchase Code") are issued by the Securities and Futures Commission (the "SFC") in consultation with the Takeovers and Mergers Panel (the "Panel") and its predecessor, the Committee on Takeovers and Mergers.

    1.2 The primary purpose of the Takeovers Code and the Share Repurchase Code (collectively, the "Codes") is to afford fair treatment for shareholders who are affected by takeover and merger transactions and share repurchases. The Codes seek to achieve fair treatment by requiring equality of treatment of shareholders, mandating disclosure of timely and adequate information to enable shareholders to make an informed decision as to the merits of an offer and ensuring that there is a fair and informed market in the shares of companies affected by takeover and merger transactions and share repurchases. The Codes also provide an orderly framework within which takeovers, mergers and share repurchases are to be conducted.

    1.3 The Codes do not have the force of law. They are framed so far as possible in non-technical language and should not be interpreted as if they are statutes. The Codes represent a consensus of opinion of those who participate in Hong Kong's financial markets and the SFC regarding standards of commercial conduct and behaviour considered acceptable for takeover and merger transactions and share repurchases in Hong Kong. This consensus of opinion is reflected in rulings made by the Panel when interpreting the Codes given the diverse range of interests represented by the Panel's members. Similar standards of commercial conduct and behaviour are applied in other leading financial centres.

---

# Page 36

**1.4** Those who wish to take advantage of the securities markets in Hong Kong should conduct themselves in matters relating to takeovers, mergers and share repurchases in accordance with the Codes. If they do not do so they may find, by way of sanction, that the facilities of such markets are withheld. The Rules Governing the Listing of Securities on The Stock Exchange of Hong Kong Limited expressly require compliance with the Codes.

**1.5** The responsibilities provided for in the Codes apply to:

- (a) directors of public companies;
- (b) persons or groups of persons who seek to gain or consolidate control of public companies;
- (c) their professional advisers; and
- (d) those who are actively engaged in the securities market in all its aspects.

**1.6** In addition, any other persons who issue circulars or advertisements to shareholders in connection with takeovers and mergers or share repurchases must observe the highest standards of care and consult with the Executive prior to the release thereof.

**1.7** The role and responsibility of financial and other professional advisers is of particular importance given the non-statutory nature of the Codes, and it is part of their responsibility to use all reasonable efforts, subject to any relevant requirements of professional conduct, to ensure that their clients understand, and abide by, the requirements of the Codes, and to co-operate to that end by responding to inquiries from the Executive Director of the Corporate Finance Division of the SFC and any delegate of the Executive Director (the "Executive"); the Panel; and the Takeovers Appeal Committee. → RIDER 1.2

## Rider 1.2

Financial advisers must therefore have the competence, professional expertise and adequate resources to fulfil their role and to discharge their responsibility under the Codes. If a financial adviser is in any doubt about its ability to meet these requirements, it should consult the Executive in advance. If the Executive considers that a financial adviser is not able to meet these requirements, it may not allow that financial adviser to act in that capacity. In such circumstances the financial adviser may ask the Panel to review the decision of the Executive.

## 2. General Principles and Rules

| 2.1 | The provisions of each Code fall into two categories. First, there are General Principles of conduct to be observed in takeover, merger or share repurchase transactions. Secondly, each Code lays down a series of Rules. Some Rules are no more than examples of the application of the General Principles and the Executive and the Panel will interpret them in accordance with those Principles. Others are rules of procedure designed to govern specific types of takeover, merger or share repurchase transactions. Accordingly, each Code, through its General Principles, may apply to a situation not specifically covered by a Rule. The Executive and the Panel may modify or relax the application of a Rule in exceptional circumstances, for example, when it considers that its strict application would operate harshly. |
| 2.2 | To assist with an interpretation of the Rules, notes have been inserted under the Rules, where appropriate, to provide guidance as to how the Rules will normally be interpreted by the Executive and the Panel. |
| 2.3 | The Executive and the Panel will interpret each Code in the light of rulings that have been made under both Codes by the Executive and the Panel, or their predecessor, the Committee on Takeovers and Mergers, and in the light of the notes that have been inserted under the rules of both Codes. |

**6.3** The Panel consists of the following members:

|  | | Members |
|---|---|---|
| (a) | Executive Directors and staff of the SFC | 4* |
| (b) | representatives of the Stock Exchange | 2 |
| (c) | representatives of financial institutions | 12 |
| (d) | representatives of other constituencies with an interest in takeovers and mergers.** | 8 |
| | TOTAL | 26 |
| | | == |

\* No more than two members of this group may sit on any referral or disciplinary hearings.

\*\* These constituencies include, among others, Non-Executive Directors of the SFC; The Law Society of Hong Kong; The Hong Kong Bar Association; The Hong Kong Society of Accountants; investment fund managers; and listed companies.

**6.4** Members of the Panel are appointed, and may be removed, by the SFC. They normally hold office for a one year term but may be reappointed at the end of each term.

---

# Page 41 (November 1992)

**6.5** The Panel may co-opt other persons to assist in specific circumstances.

**6.6** The Panel will have a Chairman, and one or more Deputy Chairmen, who may not be Executive Directors or staff of the SFC. The chairman of each hearing of the Panel will be either the Chairman or a Deputy Chairman of the Panel or, if neither is available, such other member as may be appointed by the Panel. The chairman of each hearing of the Panel will have a deliberative and a casting vote.

**6.7** Any member of the Executive appointed as a member of the Panel may not sit as a member of a panel of the Panel constituted for the purpose of considering a disciplinary matter or a matter involving an appeal from a first instance decision of the Executive.

**6.8** The quorum for the Panel is five, including a chairman.

**6.9** The Chairman will appoint a Secretary, who will normally be a staff member of the SFC.

**6.10** Each member of the Panel and, where applicable, his firm, is required to comply with conflict of interest guidelines issued by the SFC from time to time.

## 7. The Takeovers Appeal Committee

**7.1** Like the Panel, the Takeovers Appeal Committee is a committee of the SFC established under section 6(1) of the Securities and Futures Commission Ordinance (Cap. 24).

**7.2** The Takeovers Appeal Committee reviews disciplinary rulings of the Panel for the sole purpose of determining whether any sanction imposed by the Panel was unfair or excessive based upon the Panel's finding of facts.


---

# Rider 1.7

**7.3** The Chairman and Deputy Chairman of the Securities and Futures Appeals Panel are the Chairman and Deputy Chairman, respectively, of the Takeovers Appeal Committee. The other members of the Committee comprise members of the Takeovers and Mergers Panel other than representatives of the SFC, together with members of the Securities and Futures Appeals Panel who have legal training and experience.

**7.4** Each hearing of the Takeovers Appeal Committee is presided over by:–

(a) the Chairman or the Deputy Chairman; or

(b) another member of the Securities and Futures Appeals Panel who has legal training and experience and who is designated by the Chairman where he considers it inappropriate that either he or the Deputy Chairman should chair a particular hearing.

At each hearing, the Committee shall consist of three individuals, namely the person presiding over the hearing and two members of the Takeovers and Mergers Panel other than representatives of the SFC. No member of the Takeovers and Mergers Panel may participate in a hearing if he participated in the disciplinary proceedings in question.

## 8. Consulting the Executive

**8.1** When there is any doubt as to whether a proposed course of conduct is in accordance with the General Principles or the Rules, parties or their advisers should always consult the Executive in advance. In this way, the parties can obtain clarification of the basis on which they can properly proceed and thus minimise the risk of taking action which might, in the event, be a breach of one of the Codes. However, while the Executive will respond to questions respecting an interpretation of the Codes it should not be expected to answer purely hypothetical questions, or to give provisional rulings when the parties with an interest in such rulings cannot be identified.

~~**8.3**~~ **8.2** Both principals and their advisers are encouraged to make full use of this service. To take legal or other professional advice on the interpretation or application of the Codes is generally not a satisfactory alternative to obtaining a view or ruling from the Executive.

RIDER 1.7 – 8.2

---

**8.2** Consultations, which are usually initiated by parties in doubt over the applicability or interpretation of the Code, do not attract any fee, mostly take the form of verbal discussions and will not be concluded with any formal and written rulings from the Executive. As quite often only limited facts or preliminary information are provided by parties to get a quick response, views expressed by the Executive under consultations are preliminary and do not bind the Executive as rulings which are subject to review by the Panel on application. Whilst the Executive may sometimes see fit to make a ruling on a Code issue in the first instance, more often a ruling (including a decision, confirmation and determination as described in section 20.1 of the Introduction to the Code) is requested by an interested party with or without consultation, necessitating an application for a ruling in accordance with section 15.1 of the Introduction to the Code which would attract a fee. The making of a ruling by the Executive normally involves a consideration of all relevant information in relation to the application and a more thorough analysis than that permissible under a consultation. Rulings may initially be conveyed to parties orally but will always be confirmed in writing in time.

Parties should be aware that consultations will not result in provisional rulings and, where the Code requires parties to consult, they are not precluded from seeking formal rulings and a fee becomes payable whenever a ruling is sought despite the fact that the process starts as a consultation. Where there is a difference in views between the Executive and parties about a Code issue based on preliminary information during or after a consultation, the Executive, mindful of the non-binding nature of consultations, generally urges parties to seek a formal ruling with a view of preventing any breach to the Code which may result from parties taking a certain course of action against the preliminary advice of the Executive.

---

*June 1994*  *1.7*


---

# 9. Rulings by the Executive

9.1 A ruling under either Code is generally given in the first instance by the Executive. This would normally be in response to a request by an interested party. In some cases the Executive may find it necessary to convene an informal hearing or hear the views of other interested parties before making a ruling. The Executive requires prompt co-operation from those to whom enquiries are directed so that decisions may be both properly informed and given as speedily as possible.

# 10. Executive Referrals to the Panel

10.1 The Executive may refer a matter to the Panel for a ruling without itself giving a ruling when it considers that there is a particularly novel, important or difficult point at issue.

# 11. Review of Executive Decisions

11.1 If a party wishes to contest a ruling of the Executive, he may ask for the matter to be reviewed by the Panel, which can be convened at short notice. The Panel has a discretion to entertain a request for review by an aggrieved shareholder, if it is satisfied that such request is not frivolous. When the Executive considers that it is necessary to resolve an issue urgently, it may stipulate a reasonable time within which a request for review must be notified; in any other case, it must be notified at the latest within 14 days of the event giving rise to it.

# 12. Disciplinary proceedings

12.1 The Executive may institute disciplinary proceedings before the Panel when it considers that there has been a breach of either Code or of a ruling of the Executive or the Panel. ~~A disciplinary~~ **12.2 [start a new paragra**[^1] case is one the sole or main purpose of which is to propose that disciplinary action should be taken. Disciplinary action is to be distinguished from requiring compliance with, or requiring that action be taken to remedy a breach of the Code or of a ruling of the Executive or the Panel. In any such case, the Executive invites the person concerned to appear before the Panel. If the Panel finds there has been a breach of either Code or of a ruling, it may impose any of the following sanctions -

(a) private reprimand;

(b) issuance of a public statement which involves criticism;

(c) public censure;

(d) reporting the offender's conduct to the SFC or another regulatory authority (for example, the Stock Exchange, the Commissioner of Banking or any professional body) or to an overseas regulatory authority;

(e) requiring dealers and advisers, for a stated period, not to act or continue to act in any or a stated capacity for any person who has failed to comply, or has indicated that he does not intend to comply, with either Code or a ruling;

(f) banning advisers from appearing before the Executive or Panel for a stated period; and/or

(g) such other action to be taken or not taken, as it thinks fit.

The adoption of (d) above as a sanction for a breach of either Code or of a ruling does not preclude the Executive or the Panel from reporting persons to the regulatory authorities or professional bodies contemplated by (d) even though there has been no finding of such a breach if any person so reported is governed by rules, regulations or standards of professional conduct of the
